# Product checklist

**The single current work list. Restructured 19 October 2026.** Builders and agents update this file in the same increment as the work: tick an item only when its stated scope is implemented and checked on actual sessions; add new feedback here before starting it. STATUS is the session log, CONTINUITY is the handoff, numbered reviews are evidence. They are not competing task lists.

**Claiming.** Before starting an item, claim its ID in the [STATUS](STATUS.md#claimed) table and push that to `main`; `node scripts/claims.ts` shows what is open and what is taken.

**How to read it.** §1 is the **priority order**: one line per item with its ID and tags. §3 holds the **detail** for every ID, grouped by category: problem, what to do, acceptance, where in the code. §4 archives every item completed before this restructure, verbatim.

`[x]` = implemented and locally checked within the stated limits. `[ ]` = unfinished. A checked item does not certify native accounts, every device, visual preference or release readiness.

Evidence for this restructure: [122 developer review](review/122-developer-product-review.md) (with builder corrections), [123 harness data audit](review/123-harness-data-audit.md), [124 Sacred Timeline spec](review/124-sacred-timeline-git-graph.md).

**19 October investigation candidate:** [107](review/107-investigation-finishing.md) records the bounded workflow/recovery/control increment reconciled with main54f93af. New P0-P3 direction is retained. Signed-in native checks remain builder-owned; broad UI/motion/platform items are not certified by a synthetic matrix.

---

## 0. Rules for every item

1. **Product north star.** Drishya is the best *visualization of agent work* plus the *developer tools needed to understand it while it happens*. A developer should understand the change as it is made, not only after. Every item must serve that.
2. **Minimal by default.** The builder's design rule: the UI stays minimalistic and calm.
   - Do **not** add resident buttons or panels for a new feature. Put it in an existing owner (contextual hover, the ⋯ menu, Ctrl+K, a reader toolbar) unless it *replaces* something.
   - Any surface whose controls take more space than its content is a bug (see UI-5).
3. **Verify before changing.** Use the running app on actual Codex and Claude sessions and read the code before "fixing" anything. Several things that look wrong are deliberate (builder corrections, 19 Oct):
   - Toolbox→card connectors appear **on hover only**.
   - The passage layout **wraps** actions onto the next row with arrows from box to box. This is intentional; one infinite horizontal lane is rejected.
   - **File peek** (beside the agent) and **Workspace** (full view when not watching the agent) are two intended modes.
   - The **3D repository map** stays as an aesthetic surface for now.
4. **Truth.** Captured ≠ derived. A model's output is always labelled as generated and cites captured evidence. Missing data stays visibly missing. No invented links or locations ([AGENTS](../AGENTS.md) constraints).
5. **Every interaction has purposeful motion.** Every click, toggle, open and close gets a suitable, interruptible transition tied to its true origin, with reduced-motion and low-resource fallbacks (MOTION-1). Use the installed `emil-design-eng`, `animate`, `review-animations` and `animation-performance` skills.
6. **One coherent, verified increment per commit,** so either builder can backtrack. Schema changes need both builders' agreement (DATA-10).
7. **Tick, then sync.** Tick the item's own checkbox in §3, then run `node scripts/sync-tasks.ts`: it sets the ☑/☐ marks and counts in the priority tables below (`--check` verifies them).
8. **The premium bar (Yash, 29 Oct).** Every surface you touch is clean and premium down to the smallest detail, and information reaches the user with ease (no item-by-item paging, text that says what it is). Product target: $10,000 end to end, for millions of people who only read agent text today. Full statement: [VISION §8](VISION.md), *The premium bar*. Order after SCREEN-1: AUDIT-1, the UI passes, then the motion passes, COMP-1, VIZ-1.
9. **Choosing a model.**
   - Opus for design specs, ambiguous UI direction and reviews.
   - Sonnet is suitable for executing items here that have explicit **Accept** lines.
   - Either model must verify in the browser on actual sessions before ticking.

### Dependency gates and retest triggers

Keep dependent work here **and** under its own item; this is a cross-reference within the single checklist, not a second queue. “Free” in `claims.ts` means unclaimed, not necessarily ready. When a dependency merges/becomes available, the next agent checks this table, claims the affected item, reruns its stated acceptance on fresh/redacted real data and records the result. A merged dependency alone does not tick the dependent item. Keep the row until the retest passes; add newly discovered gates as work proceeds.

| Dependent item / remaining scope | Required evidence or dependency | Retest when available |
| --- | --- | --- |
| PERF-2: large-journal resource/latency gate | PERF-1 measurements; coordinate claimed DATA-1/2 ingest/store/projection changes first | Re-run complete-import and append/browser matrices after capture merges; preserve exact IDs/order and all content. Restore RSS and measured interaction budgets before new feature work; retain physical/platform checks separately. |
| READ-1 and TURN-1: Codex edit line numbers and tests hidden inside wrappers | DATA-1 typed FileChange / CommandExecution items | Re-import dense Codex session; inspect true hunk line numbers and runner outcomes, ensure wrapper/child deduplication and turn counts match captured evidence. Existing checked scope remains bounded. |
| CONTENT-9: explicit missing-reasoning state | Reasoning-state slice S3 in review125; shared contract work DATA-1/DATA-2 must be coordinated first | One quiet missing-text line for encrypted/signature-only blocks; genuine summaries/text remain visible, no line when no reasoning block was recorded; live, replay and both harnesses. |
| CONTENT-3 / CONTENT-1 / CONTENT-2: historical questions, images and web evidence | DATA-1 typed items plus review125 S4 / DATA-4; inline-image byte policy D7 needs both builders' decision | Verify pending → answered/cancelled historical states and supported native replies; mixed image/text and browser results retain exact raw access. Use a fresh sample, not tool definitions. |
| CONTENT-4: additional plan/task sources | DATA-7 and a real Claude TaskCreate/TaskUpdate/ExitPlanMode sample (review125 D10) | Ten or more captured updates show one evolving per-turn checklist, revisions remain reachable, no invented task statuses. Existing TodoWrite can be tested separately. |
| CONTENT-5: native sub-agent identities | DATA-1 / DATA-3 retained names, model, effort and lineage | Actual Codex and Claude parent/child sessions; distinguish source-linked from enclosing-call correlation, retain unresolved lineage honestly. |
| HOME-1 stats / LIVE-4 context and limits | DATA-2 usage plus DATA-3 metadata where required | Fresh real sessions and enrichment; reported vs derived labels, missing data hidden, context denominators/limit resets correct, no current-page totals mistaken for full history. |
| NATIVE-1..4 and REL-2/REL-3 broad acceptance | Real signed-in native harnesses, multi-agent/conflict samples, physical lower-resource/phone/accessibility environment | Repeat the corresponding end-to-end and freeze/truth/ten-minute checks. Synthetic transports and viewport emulation do not close these gates. |


**Tags:**
- `bug` defect
- `live` live loop / "now"
- `turn` turns and navigation
- `read` reading diffs, code and output
- `content` content-shaped presentation per action type
- `data` harness capture/adapters
- `explain` model-derived explanation
- `git` Git review, graph and timeline
- `files` Explorer, peek and Workspace
- `ui` visual system, chrome, navigation and minimalism
- `motion` interaction motion
- `home` Home and session discovery
- `review` Review work, search and packets
- `map` repository map
- `native` native controller acceptance
- `perf` performance and scale
- `rel` release and platform

**Priority:**
- **P0:** now.
- **P1:** next.
- **P2:** later.
- **P3:** deferred ideas.

**Build order (20 Oct).** Inside each table, work top to bottom and skip an item only when it is claimed or needs something that is not done. The order is by dependency, so nothing is built twice:

1. **Capture first.** DATA-1…DATA-9 change what the harnesses record; every view below stands on them.
2. **Views that consume that data.** CONTENT-*, FILES-1, REVIEW-1, HOME-1, READ-6/8, GIT-2/4, LIVE-4.
3. **Structure** (UI-1, READ-4, FILES-3…5, REVIEW-4) and native acceptance (NATIVE-*).
4. **Final passes, once, at the end:** UI-4, UI-2, UI-7, MOTION-1, MOTION-3, UI-3, UI-8, then REL-*. Do not start these while feature items above are open; polishing a surface that is still changing is done twice. Small defects found earlier are fixed in place, but the passes themselves wait.
5. **Then the P3 ideas,** including IDEA-6 (AI-written explanations, parked).

**Order after SCREEN-1 (Yash, 29 Oct).** Once the feature items are done, the order is fixed:

1. **SCREEN-1** (computer use on a big screen), with NATIVE work on whatever sessions Yash can supply. **Amendment (Yash, 30 Oct):** AUDIT-1 starts only when the remaining screen, content, files and UI items above it have landed: SCREEN-1, CONTENT-12, FILES-7, UI-1b and the claimed FILES-8, UI-16, CONTENT-8. FILES-5's remaining external platform acceptance moves with MAC-1 and does not hold the audit. **All of those landed by 31 Oct** (SCREEN-1, CONTENT-12, FILES-7, FILES-8, UI-1b, UI-16, CONTENT-8, plus CONTENT-13/14 and TEST-2's two causes); AUDIT-1 starts when Yash says. FILES-9 (an edited or written file must always open to a full view) was added on 31 Oct and is the first Pass A item.
2. **AUDIT-1:** one pass through every function that exists, as a developer would use it on real sessions, listing what is wrong in the UI, the backend handling, the displayed content and the way a user gets to information. It runs *before* any UI pass, because the UI passes would otherwise polish surfaces whose content is still unclear. It is not fitted to Yash's own list; his findings are the minimum, and what he does not name is audited too.
3. **One UI pass first** (UI-8, which absorbs UI-7, UI-3 and UI-4b: Yash, 30 Oct; [brief](review/ui-pass-brief.md)), fed by AUDIT-1's findings: layout, space, type, content presentation and every small detail settle before anything is animated.
4. **Then the motion passes** (MOTION-1, MOTION-3), on the finished UI (Yash, 29 Oct): motion is tuned to surfaces that will not move again; animating a layout that is still changing is done twice.
5. **The fresh-eyes agent review (COMP-1):** a new agent that sees the whole product as a would-be user.
6. **VIZ-1:** task-shaped visualizations and the dynamics that make it a premium end-to-end product.

First the base; the ideas in VIZ-1 are recorded now and decided after the review.

**The whole-product passes, planned (Yash, 31 Oct: "plan it your own way"; features, then UI, then motion, kept separate).** All pre-audit items have landed (SCREEN-1, CONTENT-12/13/14, FILES-7/8, UI-1b, UI-16, CONTENT-8, TEST-2's two causes). The passes run one after the other, each ending in a gate on real sessions before the next begins, because each pass hands the next one a settled surface:

1. **Pass A, features (AUDIT-1, then what it finds).** The user's-eye audit of what exists and what is missing, then the building of it, so the backend and the data are ready and complete before anything is styled. Three parts, in order:
   - **A1, the audit as a user.** For each surface, on real sessions of both harnesses at 600 and 1440 px, live and replay: what would a person using this product need here, does the feature give it, does it help or only exist, and what would help that is not there. The journeys are the unit, not the components: open a session, read what the agent did, follow one file, one command, one failure to the end, review a change, search, return later, watch a live session. Findings are whole TASKS items (features to add, features to cut or merge, backend gaps).
   - **A2, the interaction inventory.** Every component and every kind of interaction it accepts (click, double click, right click, hover, focus and Tab, keyboard keys, scroll and wheel, drag, Escape, selection and copy, resize, the same during live and replay, while loading, empty and failing), written down with what the product does for each today, on real sessions. A missing or dead interaction, an inconsistent one and one that surprises are findings. This file is the checklist the UI and motion passes must also satisfy, so nothing is redesigned without knowing what it handles.
   - **A3, build the findings** in dependency order (data and backend first, then the views that read them), each a whole item with its own acceptance, then one integrated gate.
2. **Pass B, UI (UI-8).** On the finished features: concept first per surface, then every component, page, section and state, with the interaction inventory as the checklist (hover, focus, active, disabled, empty, loading, error for each), against the premium bar, using the skills in the brief.
3. **Pass C, motion (MOTION-1, MOTION-3).** On the finished UI, from the same inventory: every interaction's transition, nothing animated that carries no information, then the ten-minute calm check on live and replay.
4. **Then COMP-1** (a fresh-eyes review of the whole), and VIZ-1.

**Not yet to the mark (Yash, 5 Nov):** after ten minutes in the app he found the composer, its menus, the session menu and Usage too basic or full of facts nobody reads, motion that opens but does not close (or the reverse), and layout that does not use large screens. The audit, UI and motion passes are not finished until every feature, component and pixel meets the premium bar; UI-27 to UI-29, CONTENT-16, CONTENT-17, CONNECT-3, LAYOUT-1 and MOTION-4 hold what he found, and AUDIT-1 looks for the rest.

**The finishing pass (Yash, 6 Nov).** The remaining audit, UI and motion work is done **one component at a time with all three lenses at once**, so each component is finished once, to the fullest: features, presentation and motion decided together, then one integrated pass over the whole. FINISH-0 (shared foundations) comes first; FINISH-1 to FINISH-12 follow in order; FINISH-13 is the integrated pass and ends with MOTION-3's evaluation and AUDIT-1's close. Each FINISH item absorbs the open items for its surface and closes them when it merges. Opus walks, decides, writes the spec and verifies; a fresh Sonnet session builds each spec. The lenses are Impeccable, `emil-design-eng` and Taste's rules, plus `break-ui` and the motion skills. Method, order, lenses and the common acceptance: [finish-pass.md](review/finish-pass.md). UI-26 is paused until FINISH-12 merges.

**Clarity (Yash, 6 Nov, later).** After seeing the Home concepts ("clean and modern and takes accurate space, minimal but professional and premium"), Yash asked for that look on every page, component, menu and dropdown, built as a **new UI version, Clarity**, with Modern kept unchanged as the backup in Settings, and full freedom over the agent visualization, themes and palettes. Every FINISH item lands in Clarity; Modern changes only through shared content logic. Clarity becomes the default at FINISH-13. Home is concept A, Ledger + Now. Architecture and the guard: [clarity-1-version.md](review/finish/clarity-1-version.md); the look: VISION §9.

**Who decides (Yash, 4 Nov):** the audit's findings and the UI and motion decisions in all three passes are Claude's to make, and AUDIT-1 is Claude's to finish: the feature audit (A1), the written interaction inventory (A2) and the building of its findings (A3). Passes B and C landed before A was finished, so AUDIT-1's remaining walk (live, the review tools, the peek, compare) and the inventory also check the finished UI and motion, and anything they find becomes a whole item.

Each pass starts by reading the ledger in the [UI pass brief](review/ui-pass-brief.md) and appending to it; findings that belong to a later pass are recorded there, not lost. A feature found missing during Pass B or C goes back to Pass A's list as an item rather than being built inside a polish pass.

**How the UI and motion passes are run (Yash, 30 Oct).** One surface at a time, in this order, so that each tool does what it is good at and nothing is polished twice:

1. **AUDIT-1's findings for that surface** are the work list; the passes do not invent their own.
2. **Reviewer lens: the HIG skill** ([dickwu/apple-design-skill](https://github.com/dickwu/apple-design-skill), 123 Apple Human Interface Guideline pages plus a design-craft lens; no licence file in the repository and the pages are Apple's text, so it is installed for the user or cloned under `.local/`, never copied into this repository). Load only the always-load set (accessibility, layout, typography, color, designing for macOS) plus the 3 to 6 pages for what is on screen (sidebars, split views, toolbars, lists and tables, search fields, windows, motion, feedback and loading states), and cite file and heading. Desktop pages only; phone, touch, widgets, Apple Pay and Duo pages are out of scope here. **Its findings are advice, filtered through our doctrine:** Apple's Liquid Glass and translucency do not override the flat, hairline theme with blur only at the header/composer edges (AGENTS creative doctrine 6), and Apple's own warning applies: delight is not decoration.
3. **Worst-case data: `break-ui`** on the surface: long names, one-letter names, thousands of items, zero items, missing fields, non-Latin text. This is the tool for the smallest things in the premium bar.
4. **Craft: `emil-design-eng`** for composition and polish, and the installed **`apple-design`** skill for how things should feel to touch (springs, interruptibility, momentum, reduced motion).
5. **Component libraries (UI-3, Uiverse and others):** only to replace a specific component the audit found weak, never to add one; copy the CSS or markup, add no dependency, check each component's licence, and run it through the freeze, truth and ten-minute tests like anything else.
6. **Then the motion pass, after the UI pass is done:** `find-animation-opportunities` to list what should move and what should not, `improve-animations` for the plan, `animate` and `review-animations` to build and check, `animation-performance` for frame budget. The AGENTS rules hold: motion carries information about origin, relationship or change, or it is cut.
7. **Verify** at 600 and 1440 px on real sessions of both harnesses.

**One pass, concept first (Yash, 30 Oct).** UI-3, UI-4b, UI-7 and UI-8 are one pass (UI-8) over **every component, page and section that comes up**, done as a professional front-end and UX designer would, not component by component in isolation. For each surface the audit answers, in writing, what it is for, what the developer must understand there, what identifies each item, what goes where and how much room it gets, which edges it needs, which controls are primary, how a window opens and what the user returns to, and what its words say (the ten questions are in the [brief](review/ui-pass-brief.md), section 3). Foundations (the token sheet, the spacing, type, radius and border scales, the anatomy of a row, a toolbar, a panel) come first so every surface is built on them. The builder is not a UI expert and expects the advanced thinking from the agent; AUDIT-1, UI-8 and the motion passes may run on Opus.

**Cross-phase ledger (Yash, 30 Oct).** What one phase finds that belongs to another (an audit finding that is a motion idea, a UI decision that changes the backend) is appended to the ledger in the [brief](review/ui-pass-brief.md), section 5, and becomes a TASKS item when it needs work. Every phase reads the ledger first and appends to it last, and thinks through the whole of AUDIT, UI and motion rather than only its own part.

**Two more skill sets for the audit, UI and motion passes (Yash, 30 Oct; read from their READMEs only, nothing installed or run yet).**

- **[Impeccable](https://github.com/pbakaus/impeccable)** (Apache-2.0, about 96,000 stars, pushed 29 Oct): a design vocabulary of 24 commands plus a deterministic detector of 62 "slop" rules (no model, no key). **Use for the audit and the UI pass, as a reviewer:** `critique` (a scored UX review that ranks what to fix first), `audit` (accessibility, performance, theming, responsive, findings ranked P0 to P3), `harden` (overflow, long text, empty and error states: the same ground as `break-ui`), `clarify` (rewrites unclear copy: our "text says what it is"), `layout` and `typeset` (spacing, hierarchy, sizes), `distill` (removes what a surface does not need), and the detector, which can scan a rendered page: `npx impeccable detect http://localhost:<port>` finds low contrast, tiny text, nested cards and gray-on-colour text on the running product. **Do not use** `bolder`, `delight`, `overdrive` or `colorize`: they push toward louder, decorated pages, against our quiet doctrine. **Cautions:** its installer writes folders for many agents into the project and can add edit hooks, and `init` writes `PRODUCT.md`; run it from a scratch worktree or user-level install and never commit what it writes (our context files are VISION, AGENTS and the brief; `document` can capture the token sheet as `DESIGN.md` once UI-8's foundations exist, and only with Yash's yes); its `live` mode hot-swaps variants into source, so use it only on a throwaway branch. Running `npx` downloads a package, so ask Yash first. Its findings are advice filtered through our doctrine, like the HIG skill's.
- **[Taste Skill](https://github.com/Leonxlnx/taste-skill)** (MIT, about 106,000 stars, pushed 30 Oct): `design-taste-frontend`, a generator that gives an agent an art direction, real pictures from open archives and a browser self-check (three widths, up to three rounds of fixing its own screenshots). It is built for marketing pages and award-style motion, which our doctrine rejects for the product. **Use for:** (1) its dashboard and app-screen route as a **reference for our rules** (quiet panels, aligned figures, colour spent on data, equal margins, a clear title on the first screen, no random blank stretches), which are the same as what the brief asks of every surface; (2) its self-check loop as a **method** (screenshot at several widths, look, fix the top problems, repeat); (3) the **launch page and the product videos** (REL-4 and VIDEO-1), where a generated, art-directed page is the point. Not for the product's own UI. Finding pictures needs network access, so keep it away from private material.

A cheap first step at the start of AUDIT-1, with Yash's yes to the download: run Impeccable's detector on the running app for a few surfaces and record the findings as audit input, then judge on those results how much weight the two sets get, as with the HIG pilot.

A pilot comes first: run steps 2 to 4 on one surface (UI-16, the Files rail) and compare the result with what the installed skills alone give, before deciding how much weight the HIG skill gets.

**Window to 18 November (Yash, 29 Oct).** Speed to market matters, because anyone can build something like this and we want to be first, and the premium bar still holds. The scope for this window runs **as far as VIZ-1's ideas**, not necessarily their implementation:

1. **Complete P2 first.** The priority is smooth, premium current things: the P2 table (the audit, the UI passes, the motion passes and the rest) before anything new.
2. **Claude Code and Codex stay the supported harnesses; Antigravity is added at Yash's request (13 Nov, P0: AG-1 to AG-5, read-only, built by Codex).** Cursor (ADAPT-1) comes only if time remains before 18 November, built from the sessions on this machine.
3. **VIZ-1:** its suggestions are thought through and started; if finished, good, otherwise it ships in the next update together with more agent support.
4. **MAC-1** (macOS acceptance) is a separate task so it is not forgotten; **VIDEO-1** (product videos) comes later.

The launch date is decided at the 18 November review, from where the list stands; no date is promised before it. Market advice recorded 29 Oct: Tuesday to Thursday, not Diwali week (8 November) and not US Thanksgiving week (26 November); the evidence for any month is anecdotal.

**Desktop only (Yash, 26 Oct).** Drishya runs on a laptop or PC against local sessions. Do not design, fix, measure or write tests for phone widths, landscape phones or touch input. The narrowest supported window is 600 px (a half-width laptop window); below it nothing is promised, and no item may be opened for it. Desktop-only also means no cloud, remote or multi-device surface.

**Real sessions, not only the demo (Yash, 26 Oct).** A change that affects what a card, reader, header, list or projection shows is checked on real local sessions of both harnesses that contain the case, as well as the fixture; record session id prefixes, sizes and counts only, keep screenshots in `.local/`, and say what could not be checked. The full rule is in AGENTS.md.

**Final passes wait (Yash, 26 Oct).** MOTION-1, MOTION-3 and UI-8 (the single UI pass; it absorbs UI-3, UI-4b and UI-7) start only when every feature item above them is done, and each is a whole pass over the finished product: look at what the content is by then, and only then decide what to change. Do not start, claim or schedule them earlier (MOTION-1 and UI-4b were started too early on 26 Oct and stopped).

**Lanes (26 Oct).** Three agents work this list at once, so each takes from its own lane first; a claim still decides, and `node scripts/claims.ts` is the first command of a session.

- **Harsh:** the reading-position jump, then DATA-3 and HOME-1 once he confirms the 26 Oct decision. PERF-2 is a final pass; REL-2 physical runs.
- **Claude (Yash's sessions):** READ-4/5/8, GIT-2, UI-1 once Yash has chosen the header, and the final UI passes. Verifies and merges its own PRs.
- **Codex (`yashdoke7`):** acceptance of the CONTENT-1…4 and CONTENT-7 readers, FILES-3/4/5, DATA-7/8/9 research, REVIEW-4. These need real samples or a native account, so they sit last in P1 rather than blocking the rows above them.
- **26 October hold lifted:** both builders confirmed deferring PERF-2/3; feature work in History/common/ingest/worker may proceed while preserving measured budgets (STATUS decisions). Active claims still apply: DATA-3 owns the turn ledger integration in `History.tsx`; coordinate that file before editing it. PERF-2/3 themselves remain deferred.

## 1. Priority order

**Overall: 115 of 193 done.** Marks are generated by `node scripts/sync-tasks.ts` from each item's checkbox below; tick the item, then run it.


### P0 — Release 1: a finished, packaged product (ship by 18 November)

**107 of 124 done**

Rebuilt by Yash with Claude, 8 November. Drishya is agent visualization: it presents what the agent is doing, faithfully and beautifully, at runtime. Release 1 ships Modern only, and its polish lands in Modern. Feature freeze at the end of 13 November; after that, only fixes from SHIP-10 and the gate. Done items are no longer listed in these tables; their details stay below. Where a detail line names another priority, this table wins.

**Execution order (Opus, 11 Nov).** Rows are in the order to build them; done items follow. Each phase starts only when the one before it allows, and items inside a phase never share files.
- **A · building now, in parallel:** UI-28c (Claude: `PromptRail.tsx`, `SessionWorkspace.tsx`, `composer.css`) · LINK-1 (Claude: prose links and hover cards, `ProseFileLink.tsx`, `markdown.tsx`, `BrowserEvidence.tsx`, `git-review.ts`, `server.ts`, `api.ts`) · LAYOUT-1b redo (Claude: `interface-scale.ts`, `ReadingWindow.tsx`, `large-screens.css`, the large-screen and passage specs) · SHIP-20 (Codex: colour and weight only, its own `reader-ink.css`).
- **B · next, independent of A:** SHIP-16 (CI workflow only; the release-candidate run and the WSL2 walk come again in E) · SHIP-21 (after SHIP-20, same pattern; Opus decides Midnight) · SHIP-3 (**deferred by Yash, 11 Nov; draft #411 only**). · FILES-15 (Files tree and Git root choice: `file-roots.ts`, `worktree-files.ts`, the Files part of `SessionWorkspace.tsx`; already active, finish independently; SHIP-16a next, before LAYOUT-1c/MOTION-4). · LAYOUT-1c (`large-screens.css` stage variables, the `ReadingWindow.tsx` stage read and one small script hook; after FILES-15, before MOTION-4). · SHIP-2e (Claude, after Codex's limit: `FencedCode.tsx`, its CSS and `highlight.tsx` only). · SHIP-16a (Codex, right after SHIP-21: daemon grant and path code, their tests, CI only; no UI files).
- **B2 · added to P0 by Yash, 11 Nov (requested, never marked later):** FILES-16 (Codex, right after FILES-15: the same `file-roots.ts`, `worktree-files.ts` and Files part of `SessionWorkspace.tsx`) · LIVE-7 (daemon live path and page timing instrumentation only; no UI files). CONNECT-4, CONNECT-4b and STORE-1 moved to P1 by Yash the same day (first after the release). ## 2. Status snapshot

- **Workspace editing:** Workspace → select file → **Edit file** → **Compare draft** → **Review edit with harness**. Drafts are memory-only; disk changes only when the user submits a request and the native harness applies it. There is no direct save-to-disk.
- **Works today:** live capture and history; real diffs, outputs and test bars; file peek; Workspace drafts; Git review; Git branch atlas; cross-session search; Review work; Ctrl+K; seven palettes; native controller (local fixtures).
- **Measured on a 1,100-operation Codex session:** 2.3 s load, about 2.4k DOM nodes, 15 MB heap, about 7% idle main-thread time. Live latency is about 2 s.
- **Biggest gaps:** no "now"; no turn chapters; narrow wrapped diffs; half the harness data unused ([123](review/123-harness-data-audit.md)); reasoning mostly unrecorded by the harnesses themselves; the Git graph is art without information.

## 3. Detail by category

### SHIP — Release 1 (8 to 18 November)

Decided by Yash with Claude, 8 November: [release-1-scope.md](review/release-1-scope.md). Release 1 ships Modern; Release 1 polish lands in Modern; feature freeze at the end of 13 November. Accept every item on real sessions of both harnesses at 600 and 1440 px unless it says otherwise. Claim first; one builder per file at a time (SHIP-4, SHIP-5, SHIP-7 and SHIP-12 share `History.tsx`/`SessionWorkspace.tsx`; SHIP-2, SHIP-8 and MOTION-4 share stylesheets).

- [x] **SHIP-1 · Modern only** · P0 · `ui` `rel` — Yash, 8 Nov: the UI-version choice is unfinished and must not reach users.
  - **Do:** remove the UI-versions tab and its developer switch from Settings, and remove every route by which Modern, Legacy or Clarity can be selected in the product (URL parameter, stored preference, keyboard). A stored non-Modern preference falls back to Modern silently. The packaged build loads only Modern; Clarity's sources stay in the repository, unreachable from the product. Remove or update the specs and the clarity-guard capture that select versions; keep Modern's pixels unchanged.
  - **Accept:** a fresh package and an existing home with a stored Clarity or Legacy preference both open in Modern with no version wording anywhere in Settings or menus; a search of the built UI bundle finds no version chooser strings; `package:verify` passes.
  - **Checked 8 Nov, PR #369 (other-builder review pending):** packaged fresh/existing scratch homes and old preferences open only Modern, with five Settings sections; both genuine harnesses at 600/1440 px; four final history frames have zero changed pixels against main; package verification and related types/45 units pass. Version/controller/reading test migrations are complete. The broad browser snapshot is red; three Modern motion expectations reproduce on pre-change production code and are recorded for Claude/TEST-2 in [the verification record](review/ship-1-modern-only.md#claude-investigation). No all-green release gate is claimed.
- [x] **SHIP-2a · Palettes and semantic colour tokens** · P0 · `ui` — release umbrella for SHIP-17 through SHIP-21.

SHIP-2b, 2c and 2d remain open for their own frame reviews.

- [x] **GIT-10 · Git graph opens at once and is kept** · P0 · `git` `perf` — Yash, 12 Nov: "git graph takes a lot of time to render, and always renders again and again, not like saves it for next time."
  - **Cause (Opus, from the code):** the daemon's `git-history.ts` runs `git log` and builds the page on every request (only commit signatures are cached), although every page already carries a `generation` hash of refs and HEAD. The page's `GitHistoryReader` keeps everything in component state and is unmounted on close, so each open starts from nothing and lays the graph out again.
  - **Do:**
    1. **Daemon:** cache history pages per repository root by (generation, reference, query, start), bounded in size (least recently used), dropped when the generation changes. Checking the generation is cheap (refs and HEAD); only a changed generation reruns `git log`.
    2. **Page:** keep the last pages and the computed graph layout per recording and checkout outside the component, so reopening paints at once with the same scroll position and selection. Revalidate the generation in the background; if Git changed, update once quietly (no flash, no re-run of arrivals), keeping the selected commit when it still exists.
    3. **Measure:** time from click to first painted graph, cold and warm, and the layout time, on this repository and a real large one.
  - **Accept:** on this repository (it has many branches) and one large real repository: warm reopen paints in under 150 ms, cold open time recorded before and after in `docs/review/`, no change in what the graph shows (same commits, lanes and order as before), a new commit or branch change shows after reopening without a manual refresh. Units for the daemon cache (hit, generation change, bound); a browser spec for reopen-without-refetch.

Dated decision-log entries in STATUS stay as history.
  - **Accept:** `git grep -i -E "nebius|nemotron|NEBIUS_API_KEY|tokenfactory"` finds nothing outside dated history; the CLI starts without a Model line; the SinceMark pill and every other surface still work without the briefing (it either opens the existing evidence view or is removed, decided in the PR); all units and the affected browser specs pass.
- [x] **DATA-14 · The history worker waits for its cache lock** · P0 · `data` `bug` — Yash, 12 Nov: `pnpm serve` logged `Error: database is locked` from `history.ts` (the worker's error event) right after start.
  - **Cause (Opus, from the code):** `history-worker.ts` opens `history/index.db` and runs `PRAGMA journal_mode=WAL` and its `CREATE TABLE` statements with no `busy_timeout`, while the main store sets `busy_timeout = 3000`. Any overlap on that file (a previous daemon still closing, or a replacement worker starting while the old one holds a write) fails at once instead of waiting.
  - **Do:** set `PRAGMA busy_timeout` (about 5 s) as the worker's first statement; make sure a replacement worker is created only after the previous one has exited; log one clear line if the lock is still held after the wait.
  - **Accept:** a unit that holds a write on `index.db` while a second worker starts, and the second worker waits then succeeds; `pnpm serve` twice in a row on a real home shows no lock error.

- [x] **UI-30 · Focus rings only where they help** · P0 · `ui` — Yash, 12 Nov, with screenshots: a lavender ring around the Files picker's summary, and a vertical ring line down the left edge of the agent visualization. "It sometimes comes up, and sometimes doesn't ... looks way bad ... for some places it does look good."
  - **Cause (Opus, from the code):** about 145 separate `:focus`/`:focus-visible` rules in two styles (2 px `--focus` lavender and 1 px `--t2`). `:focus-visible` also matches programmatic focus once the person has used the keyboard, and many surfaces return focus to their trigger on close (`trigger.focus()`), so a ring appears after a mouse action whenever any key was pressed earlier. That is the "sometimes". Large focusable regions (scrollers with `tabIndex`) draw a ring around the whole region.
  - **Do:**
    1. Track the last input modality (pointer or keyboard) on the root. After a pointer action, programmatic focus draws no ring; keyboard navigation always does.
    2. Regions and scrollers (history, readers, diff and result viewports, Workspace panes) never draw a ring around themselves. If a region needs a keyboard focus cue, it is a quiet inner indicator, not an outline at the edge.
    3. One ring for controls, shared through one token: thin, neutral (`--t2`-family) and inset or tightly offset, in all three palettes. The lavender 2 px ring goes, except where the builders keep it on purpose (list them in the PR).
    4. Keep keyboard accessibility: every control still shows a visible ring when reached by Tab, arrows or shortcuts.
  - **Accept:** the two screenshot cases reproduced on real sessions (type in the composer, then click the picker and close it; then click into history): no ring after the mouse actions, a ring on Tab. A list in the PR of every focus rule removed or merged. 600 and 1440, three palettes. A browser spec covering pointer-after-keyboard, keyboard-only and a region scroller.

Long prompts still clamp and expand as today; attachments and images stay. Freeze test: any paused frame reads prompt first, metadata second.
  - **Accept:** before/after frames on real Claude Code and Codex sessions (short, long, with an image, running and finished) at 600 and 1440 in all three palettes, in `.local/`; the existing turn specs pass in what they assert. Opus judges the frames at review.

- [x] **SEC-3 · Threat model and an automated security suite** · P0 · `privacy` `rel` — Yash, 12 Nov: "add tests, security related, penetration testing and tests that we should and have to run for the product ... just before the packaging ... api and stuff so we dont cause any issue in privacy or hacks or leaking any information or loose ends anywhere, cause we are working locally and it has internet access ... also its on ports."
  - **Where it sits (Yash, 13 Nov: "sec before ship9? ... sec 3 so early in the list?").** After the UI work and just before SHIP-9, as he first asked on 12 Nov, so the suite tests the product that ships. The one exception: the five checks under *To verify first* take minutes each and can be run at any time; if one turns out real, its fix does not wait for the rest of this item.
  - **What is being protected.** Drishya holds a developer's prompts, source, command output and diffs, reads their repositories, can run `git` and (opted in) `gh`, and (opted in) can send a prompt to a native harness. It serves all of that from a local HTTP port to a browser that is also on the internet.
  - **Who attacks, and from where:**
    1. A web page the person visits while Drishya runs (requests from their browser to the port, DNS rebinding, a framed or popped-up Drishya).
    2. Content inside what Drishya shows: a prompt, tool output, a file name, a branch name, a commit message, an image, an MCP result, a repository's own Git configuration.
    3. Another program or user on the same machine (the port, the cookie, the store on disk, the launch link).
    4. A device on the same network.
    5. The package and its dependencies.
  - **Already in place (read in `apps/daemon/src/server.ts`, 12 Nov; each still needs a test that would fail without it):** loopback-only bind that refuses any other host; a per-launch 192-bit token exchanged for an `HttpOnly; SameSite=Strict` cookie, the token carried in the URL fragment; `Host` and `Origin` checks on every request; `application/json` required on writes; `nosniff`, `X-Frame-Options: DENY`, `frame-ancestors 'none'`, `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`, a `default-src 'self'` CSP; redaction before storing (SEC-2); no telemetry; zero runtime dependencies in the daemon.
  - **To verify first, found while writing this (Fable, 12 Nov; none confirmed yet):**
    1. `git` and `gh` are started by bare name with the repository as working directory (`git-review.ts`, `git-history.ts`, `github-links.ts`). On Windows a bare name can resolve to a file in the working directory before `PATH`, so a repository containing `git.exe` could get it run. Test with a harmless marker program; if it runs, resolve the absolute path once at start.
    2. Browser cookies are not scoped to a port: the session cookie for `127.0.0.1:7878` is also sent to any other local web server on `127.0.0.1`. Test what such a server receives and whether it can replay it; if so, bind the session to something that server cannot use.
    3. The CSP allows `style-src 'unsafe-inline'`. Check what captured content could do with injected styles (hide or restyle the screen, load nothing external).
    4. `packages/explain` makes outbound requests when a key is set. CLEAN-2 removes it; until then the outbound test must prove it is silent without a key.
    5. A repository's own Git configuration can make `git` run programs (`core.fsmonitor`, `diff.external`, `textconv`, `core.sshCommand`, pager, hooks). Confirm every call disables them.
  - **Do:** write `docs/review/sec-3-threat-model.md` (assets, attackers, entry points, what each defence is and its test), and a suite in `tests/security/` run by `pnpm test:security`, in CI on all three systems and inside `package:verify`. No test prints a secret or real journal content.
    1. **Network exposure.** The process listens on loopback only (the listening address is asserted; a connection to the machine's LAN address is refused); `--host` with any non-loopback value is refused; exactly one port is open; nothing listens on a wildcard or IPv6 any-address.
    2. **The request gate, for every route.** Build the route list from the server itself, so a new route cannot be missed. For each route and method: no token gives 401; a wrong `Host` gives 421; a foreign `Origin` gives 403; no response ever carries an `Access-Control-Allow-*` header; a preflight is refused; a write sent as `text/plain` or as a form is refused; the event stream and image routes need the same session. The token compare is constant-time; the token never appears in a query string, a log line, a `Referer` or the store. Sign-out ends the session.
    3. **File access.** Every route that takes a path, a ref or an id is tried with `..`, absolute paths, drive letters, UNC paths, URL-encoded and double-encoded forms, a null byte, symlinks and junctions that leave the checkout, NTFS alternate streams and device names. Only granted checkouts are readable; protected files stay protected; no route writes into a project or a harness directory; journals are opened read-only.
    4. **Injected content.** A corpus of hostile strings and files (script tags, event handlers, `javascript:` and `file:` links, SVG with script, HTML in Markdown and in MCP results, ANSI and bidi control characters, very long tokens) goes through the real ingest path and is rendered in History, Files, Changes, the Git graph, search, notes and exports. Nothing executes, no request leaves the page, no link has a scheme other than `http` or `https`, external links carry `rel="noopener noreferrer"`, images are served with their real type and `nosniff`, and a CSP violation listener records zero reports.
    5. **Command execution.** Every place a program is started (`git`, `gh`, a harness, opening a file) uses an argument list and no shell; values from captured data follow `--`; a ref or path that starts with `-` cannot become an option; the executable cannot come from the repository; Git runs with hooks, fsmonitor, external diff, textconv, pager and credential prompts off; no Git command touches the network.
    6. **Outbound traffic.** With default settings, a full walk (Home, a session, Files, Changes, the Git graph, search, Settings, an export) is run with the daemon's and the page's requests recorded: nothing leaves loopback. Each opt-in (`gh`, the GitHub link) is listed with exactly what it sends and when. No fonts, scripts or images load from another host; there is no update check.
    7. **Secrets and private data.** A journal seeded with one fake secret per redaction rule is imported; the store, blobs, previews, the search index, exports, the offline bundle, logs and crash output are then searched for the raw values: none. The launch token and the session id are not written to disk. Exports say whether they contain local paths, and mask them when asked.
    8. **Limits.** Oversized bodies and headers, slow requests, deep JSON, many open event streams, one enormous journal line and an image bomb are each refused or bounded, and the daemon stays responsive. Every redaction rule is run against adversarial input with a time limit, so no rule can hang ingest.
    9. **In the browser.** What Drishya keeps in `localStorage`, `sessionStorage` and IndexedDB is listed and holds no token and no journal content beyond what the feature needs; API responses that carry captured content are `no-store`; there is no service worker.
    10. **Sending prompts (the opt-in controller).** A prompt, an interrupt and an approval can only follow a person's action in Drishya's own page: each is tried from another origin and without the consent state, and refused. An approval answers one exact request once.
  - **Accept:** the threat model reviewed by both builders; `pnpm test:security` green on Windows, Linux and macOS; each defence listed above has a test that fails when the defence is removed (shown once in the PR by removing it); the five items under *To verify first* each end as a fix with a test or as a written reason it is not exploitable.
- [ ] **SEC-4 · Penetration pass on the release candidate** · P0 · `privacy` `rel` — Yash, 12 Nov (with SEC-3). The automated suite proves what we thought of; this pass looks for what we did not, on the exact build that will ship (after SEC-3, on SHIP-9's candidate, before SHIP-10).
  - **Do, against the packed candidate installed the way a user installs it, with real sessions of both harnesses in a scratch home:**
    1. **A hostile page.** A page on another origin, open in the same browser, tries to read and change Drishya: `fetch` with and without credentials, forms, images, `EventSource`, WebSocket, a frame, a pop-up with `window.opener`, timing of responses, and guessing the port.
    2. **DNS rebinding.** A real rebinding test (for example NCC Group's Singularity) against the running port.
    3. **A scanner and a fuzzer.** OWASP ZAP (baseline, then an authenticated active scan) and a fuzzer over the route list (methods, parameters, bodies, headers, encodings).
    4. **The network.** `nmap` from another device on the same network and from WSL2 or a container on the same machine; a check of what VS Code, WSL or Docker port forwarding would expose and what the docs should say.
    5. **Another local user or program.** A second account on the machine tries the port, the store directory, the temp files and the launch link (process list, shell history, terminal scrollback). File permissions of the store are checked on all three systems.
    6. **A hostile repository and a hostile journal.** A repository built to attack (a planted `git.exe`, hostile Git config, hooks, symlinks out of the tree, huge and oddly named files) and a hand-made journal with hostile content are opened and walked through every surface.
    7. **Static analysis.** CodeQL and Semgrep over the repository; every `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`, `innerHTML`, `eval`, `new Function`, `child_process` and file-system call that takes outside input is read by a person.
  - **Accept:** `docs/review/sec-4-pentest.md` lists every tool, version, command and finding with a severity; each finding is fixed with a test added to `tests/security/`, or accepted with a reason both builders sign; nothing rated high is open at release. No real journal content or secret appears in the report.
- [x] **SEC-5 · Supply chain and release hygiene** · P0 · `privacy` `rel` — Yash, 12 Nov (with SEC-3). What ships, and what becomes public.
  - **Do:**
    1. **Package contents.** `npm pack` is compared with an allowlist in a test: no `.env`, `.local`, journals, real fixtures, screenshots, store files or editor settings; source maps carry no absolute local paths; there is no install script.

    3. **Git history, before PUBLIC-1.** The whole history of every branch is scanned for secrets and for real journal content (gitleaks and trufflehog, plus SEC-2's own rules run over every blob). Anything found is rotated first and then removed from history before the repository is public.
    4. **Reporting and docs.** `SECURITY.md` with how to report a problem and what is in scope; the README's privacy section states exactly what Drishya reads, stores, sends (nothing by default) and listens on, and warns against exposing the port through a proxy or port forwarding.
    5. **Release checklist.** A written list run at every release: `pnpm test:security`, the package allowlist, the audit, the history scan, the outbound-traffic walk on the built package, and the version of each tool used.

  - **Done 15 Nov (Yash with Claude Sonnet 5.5).** `scripts/lib/package-contents.ts` (allowlist, manifest, absolute-path, redaction-rule and source-map checks) runs in `scripts/verify-package.ts`; `tests/security/11-package.test.ts` (49 tests) proves each rule fails when its trigger appears and asserts the zero-dependency closure; the packed server also runs with `scripts/lib/net-recorder.mjs` preloaded and `package:verify` fails on any non-loopback connect, lookup or UDP send. `pnpm audit`: no known vulnerabilities; Dependabot: 0 open (19 fixed). Provenance is configured (`publishConfig`, `.github/workflows/release.yml`, manual and unrun; the npm trusted publisher is still to be set up on npmjs.com by a builder). **History scan: no real secret or journal content**; findings for the builders (personal paths and ids in the notes, older footage images) are in the [report](review/sec-5-history-scan.md) and favour REPO-1's fresh history. `SECURITY.md`, the README privacy section and [`docs/release-checklist.md`](release-checklist.md) are in. - [ ] **SEC-6 · A session credential that another local web server cannot replay** · P1 · `privacy` — Fable and Claude Sonnet 5.5, 14 Nov (found by SEC-3, [threat model](review/sec-3-threat-model.md) §5 item 2). The `drishya_session` cookie has no port scope (RFC 6265), so the browser also sends it to every other web server on `127.0.0.1` or `localhost` that the same browser visits; one that logs request headers could replay it. Accepted for release 1: a replayer must be a program on this machine, the Host check refuses anything else, and a program running as the same account can already read the store.
  - **Do:** bind the session to something a different server on another port cannot present: a second secret held by the page and sent in a header, or a per-port cookie name plus a header proof. It must also cover requests that carry no script-set header (`<img>` for images, `EventSource` for `/v1/changes`: via same-origin fetch to blob URLs, or a short-lived one-use URL), and keep the Playwright specs that call the API with the page's cookie jar working.
  - **Accept:** `tests/security/00-verify-first.test.ts` item 2 turns from "pins the recorded risk" into "a server on another port that holds the cookie cannot replay it"; `pnpm test:security` green on all three systems.

- [x] **GIT-11 · Squash-merged branches join main in the Git graph** · P0 · `git` `ui` — Yash, 12 Nov: "the git graph never merges, it just leaves it there itself, cant we join it?" Every PR here is squash-merged, so Git records no merge link and each merged branch ends in the air beside main.
  - **Today (Opus, from the code):** `git-light.ts` already draws a squash join, but only when a newer primary commit wrote *exactly* the branch head's file contents (`GitThreadGraph.tsx`, `squash`). That almost never holds here: before merging, main is merged into the PR branch (docs resolved), so the branch head's change and the squash commit differ.
  - **Do:** find the squash commit by stronger, still evidence-based matches, in this order, and draw the join with the existing squash edge and its "squash-merged into main at … (Git has no merge link)" note:
    1. The branch's own commits (ignoring merges from main) have the same patch content as one primary commit (`git patch-id` of the branch's net own diff against the squash commit's diff), or the squash commit changes a superset of the paths with identical contents for every path the branch's own commits changed.
    2. When GitHub lookups are on (the existing opt-in through the user's `gh`), a merged PR's head branch and merge commit name the pair exactly; show `#N` in the note.
    3. The squash commit's subject ends in `(#N)` and that PR's head branch is the local branch (only with the same opt-in).
    Never join on a name or time guess alone. A branch with no match stays unjoined, as now. The join works when the squash commit is on a later page than the branch tip (the evidence already crosses loaded pages).
  - **Accept:** on this repository, at least the branches of #419 to #427 (gemini/*, claude/*) join main at their squash commits with GitHub lookups off where patch evidence suffices, and all of them with lookups on; branches never merged stay unjoined; units for each match rule and a non-match; the Git graph spec updated; 600 and 1440 in all three palettes.

Reading edges, not black bands (Yash, 13 Nov: "the header part is blacked out as well as near the agent, that is not what i wanted, that was purposefully done in that way to give the agent visualization the main thing").** SHIP-6e (#437) painted solid page black across the whole window under the header and above the composer (measured 60 and 64 px at 1440, 132 and 305 px at 600), hiding the ground and the work that runs under the floating controls by design. **Do:** no paint over the world; History's own content fades to a tenth under the measured chrome, so no text is readable against a control (SHIP-6e's measure stays). - [ ] **UI-40 · More palettes, each designed as a whole system** · P1 · `ui` — **P1, next release (Yash, 14 Nov: "move map 3 and ui 40 in p1"; earlier "rather than ui40, we go for this plans").** Earlier the same day: just before SHIP-9, after most of the work is done; Yash finalises from a frames demonstration.** Yash, 13 Nov: "i also want to add more themes, right now there are just 3, i want more, and rather than more quantity, i want more of quality as well, black and white look class, but maybe if we can get more color palette ... that will look attractive, and dont just think of it as just normal colors, that wine red looks good, no, according to what we are showing, developers and their use and colors that will look as a whole color palette on the product is a necessary relation we have to see."
  - **Today:** three palettes (Jet Black, Midnight, Paper) in `apps/ui/src/tokens.css`, under SHIP-17's palette contract and SHIP-21's paint policy (every colour is a token).
  - **What a palette is here.** Not a background and an accent. Each one carries, as one designed set: the ground and three surface steps; three line steps; four ink steps; the accent and its partner with hover, pressed and tint; the meaning colours (read, edit, pass, fail, warning); the agent inks (Claude Code, Codex, sub-agents); seven syntax colours; the diff's added and removed grounds and inks; selection, focus and search-hit; the brand ink; and how captured images and screenshots sit on it.
  - **The rule that decides which hues are allowed.** The product's content already owns red (failed, removed), green (passed, added), amber (edited) and blue (read), plus the two agent inks. A palette's own hue must stay clear of all of them, or a diff, a failure or an agent stops reading at a glance. So a wine-red or a forest-green ground is out however good it looks alone; neutral, cream, slate, indigo and violet families are open. Measured, not judged by eye: a minimum OKLab distance between the accent and each meaning colour and agent ink (UI-34 used 0.06), and between every pair of meaning colours on that palette's own surfaces.
  - **Do:**
    1. **A black-and-white class first** (Yash's "black and white look class"): one dark and one light palette with no hue of their own at all: pure neutral ground, lines and ink, the accent is the ink itself. The only colour on screen is meaning (pass, fail, edit, read, the agents) and syntax, so it reads strongest of all.
    2. **Three or four colour candidates** from the open families, each a full token set, for example a warm graphite with the site's cream ink, a deep indigo, a cool slate, and a warm light paper. These are candidates to test, not decisions.
    3. **Judge them on the product, not on swatches.** For every candidate, the same real screens are rendered side by side: History with a diff, a failed command and both agents; Files with a source file open; Changes; the Git graph; Settings; a dialog; Home. Yash picks from those frames; a candidate that fails any gate below is dropped, not tuned by eye.
    4. **Settings:** each palette gets a preview card drawn from its own tokens, a name, and one line on what it is for. Jet Black stays the default.
  - **Gates for every palette that ships:** text 4.5:1 and controls 3:1 on each surface step; the hue-distance rule above; all seven syntax colours 4.5:1 on the code ground and distinct from each other; added and removed still distinct under a red-green colour-vision simulation (the sign and position carry it, the colour must not undo it); captured images framed neutrally; `palette-contrast` and the paint policy extended to the new palettes; real sessions of both harnesses at 600 and 1440.
  - **After UI-39:** it changes Jet Black's ink and button shapes; new palettes are built on the settled tokens so nothing is designed twice.
  - **Yash, 13 Nov, before it starts:** "white is way too bright, black looks rich because of different color and background and border and stuff for clear distinction ... we have to consider those as well for color themes for all." So every palette, light ones above all, is judged on its *steps*, not its hue: the ground, three surface steps and three line steps must separate as clearly as Jet Black's do (measure the step contrasts on Jet and hold every palette to them), and a light palette's ground is never pure white (an off-white paper with ink that is not pure black, so a long reading session does not glare). Paper is redone under the same rule.
  - **Yash's candidates, 13 Nov** ("if it looks good on main product on various types of pages and keep the best ones"). Every one is built as a full token set and rendered on the same real screens as step 3; the best are kept, the rest dropped. Dark ground / light ground / accent:

    | Candidate | Dark | Light | Accent | Meant to feel |
    |---|---|---|---|---|
    | Espresso & Oat | `#16120E` | `#F5EFE6` | `#D4A373` | warm, paper-like, no glare |
    | Oxblood & Parchment | `#1F0D12` | `#F7F2EB` | `#E5B8B8` | editorial, bold |
    | Smoked Slate & Terracotta | `#1C1D21` | `#F4EBE6` | `#E07A5F` | neutral charcoal warmed by clay |
    | Obsidian & Phosphor Amber | `#11100E` | `#FFF8EB` | `#F59E0B` (or `#FFB000`) | retro terminal, hardware |
    | Graphite & Oscilloscope Sage | `#121615` | `#EAF0ED` | `#8AB8A8` | instrument panel, drafting paper |
    | Braun Industrial | `#181818` | `#E8E6DF` | `#EA580C` (or `#FF5722`) | matte, putty, one orange mark |

For each, try the accent as given first; if it fails the gate, show the frame anyway with the nearest passing accent beside it (or, where only one meaning colour collides, that palette's own shifted meaning colour), so Yash judges the real trade-off rather than a silent substitution. The light grounds are all off-white, which already meets his "white is too bright" rule.

  - **Today:** History interleaves prompts, the agent's messages, recorded reasoning and passages of tool cards. In a long turn two of the agent's messages can be several screens of cards apart, so following its account of the work means scrolling past the work.
  - **Do:** a second way to read History, *Conversation*: prompts, the agent's messages and recorded reasoning in order, and each run of tool cards between two messages folded to the one summary line History already writes for it (read 4 · searched 2 · edited 3 files (+19 −5) · 5 commands · 1 failed · 1 m 25 s). Clicking a line opens that run's cards in place and keeps the reading position; it closes the same way. The map, the Toolbox and capture keep working underneath: in a live turn the run in progress shows its line with real counts as events arrive and folds when the next message lands.
  - **Where the switch lives (Fable's recommendation; Yash decides from frames):** one two-state control, *Work · Conversation*, in the header's view cluster beside the layout button, because it is a way of reading History, like the layout; plus a key. The choice is remembered per window. Each folded line is itself the way back to the work at that point, so seeing one run never needs the switch.
  - **Build notes (for the Sonnet session; Fable, 13 Nov):**
    1. **A run** is the consecutive tool operations between two pieces of the agent's text (prose or recorded reasoning) inside one turn; a prompt, an agent message or a compaction seam ends a run. Find how History groups operations into passages (`apps/ui/src/components/History.tsx`) and build runs from the same data, not from the DOM.
    2. **The line** reuses the turn ledger's wording (`apps/ui/src/lib/turn-ledger.ts`: `read 4 · searched 2 · edited 3 files (+19 −5) · 5 commands · 1 failed · 1 m 25 s`), computed per run instead of per turn: add a per-run ledger beside the per-turn one, with units in `apps/ui/test/`.
    3. **State:** one `historyMode: 'work' | 'conversation'` in the app store (`apps/ui/src/state.ts`), remembered per window in the existing workspace memory; open runs in conversation mode are a set of run ids, also per window.
    4. **The switch:** a two-state segmented control in the header's view cluster next to the layout button (`.studio-actions`), labelled *Work* and *Conversation*, with a key (pick one free in the keyboard map in Settings › Keyboard and list it there). It uses the existing pill buttons (UI-39 part 2).
    5. **Rendering:** in conversation mode a folded run renders as one quiet row (the line, the run's failure mark if any, a chevron); clicking it renders that run's passages exactly as Work mode does, in place. Keep the paged history and its measured heights correct: folding changes row heights, so go through the history's own measurement path, never hide rows with CSS alone.
    6. **Live:** the run in progress shows its line updating as operations land; when the next message lands it folds.
    7. **Motion:** opening and closing a run is one height and opacity transition of the real rows (MOTION rules, reduced motion off), interruptible.
    8. **Checks:** typecheck, the new units, `history-reader`, `conversation-chat`, `ship-2f-history` and `large-screens` specs, plus a new `conversation-view.spec.ts`; real Claude and Codex sessions in a scratch home at 1440, 2000 and 600; frames in `.local/ui-41/`. Open a draft PR after the first commit; Fable reviews.
  - **Built (Claude Sonnet 5.5, 13 Nov, PR #460):** `conversation-runs.ts` builds runs from the history's passages and folds them through the same measured-height path as a folded turn (hidden passages measure zero, folded ones keep their own `#run` height); `run-ledger.ts` writes the line with the turn ledger's wording (`tallyOperation` is shared with `turns.ts`, so they cannot count differently); `historyMode` is in the store and the window's memory (`@home`, key `history/mode`), open runs per session (`history/open-runs`); the switch is in the header cluster beside Workspace and Git, key `v` (listed in the reading shortcuts).
  - **Not:** no written summary and no model text (captured ≠ derived): the line is counts and outcomes only; a failure is never hidden (a run with a failed command keeps its failure mark on its line); nothing leaves search, the prompt rail or replay.
  - **Accept:** real sessions of both harnesses (long turns, sub-agents, a compaction, a failed run) at 1440 and 2000, plus 600: in Conversation every agent message of a turn is reachable with no card between them; opening and closing a run keeps the reading position; Follow latest, Back to latest, the prompt rail and replay work in both modes; switching modes moves cards into their lines and back (MOTION); browser spec for the fold, the failure mark and the remembered choice; Yash accepts from frames.
- [x] **UI-41b · Conversation mode keeps every row while scrolling** · P0 · `ui` `bug` — Yash, 13 Nov, on UI-41 (#460): in Conversation mode, scrolling up in a long session, "loading session" sometimes flashes in and parts of the conversation are missing or blank until scrolled again; it happens often.
  - **Where to look:** folding changes row heights, so the paged history's measured heights (`history/heights`, the `#run` keys), its anchor restore and its page loader (`onWindow`, the "Loading session…" slot) can fall out of step with the folded rows. Reproduce first with a spec.
  - **Do:** a long session-sized fixture in Conversation mode, wheel-scrolled up quickly through several pages, asserting that no loading state shows for pages already loaded, that every prompt and agent message in the scrolled range is rendered, and that the anchor holds. Then fix the cause.
  - **Cause and fix (Claude Sonnet 5.5, 13 Nov, PR #471):** folded rows are short, so a screen holds many passages and the loader (32 per page, 900 px look-ahead, a new request on every scroll step, each making the one in flight stale) was outrun and starved. In Conversation the loader now asks for the largest page (64), placed to cover about two screens each way with the spare room toward the scroll direction; it does not ask again while a request in flight covers what is missing in view; and unloaded rows stand in with the measured height of folded rows, not a Work-mode passage's. New `conversation-scroll.spec.ts` (a 700-turn, 11,900-observation fixture): before, 60% of frames showed a loading slot and 179 page requests; after, 0 to 2% and 8 to 26 requests.
  - **Accept:** that spec red before and green after; `conversation-view`, `b4-history`, `ship-2f-history` and `passage-layout` pass; real Claude Code and Codex sessions in a scratch home at 1440, scrolled up in Conversation mode, show no loading flash and no missing row (counts only).
- [x] **UI-41d · Switching from Work to Conversation keeps the reading place too** · P0 · `ui` `bug` — Yash, 14 Nov, after UI-41c (#478) merged: "now toggling from conversation to work keeps the page the same but the other way it doesnt."
  - **Where to look:** UI-41c's mode-switch anchor in `apps/ui/src/components/History.tsx` (`switching`, `modeAnchor`, the subscribe layout effect that captures the anchor before re-render and restores it after). In Work the top-of-view anchor can be an ops row or a run line inside a passage, which has no matching element in Conversation; find what the capture picks in Work and what the restore looks for in Conversation, on a real session, before changing anything.
  - **Do:** from Work, map the anchor to the nearest element Conversation renders for the same passage (its turn, its prose, or the operation's line in the conversation's run) and restore its offset; never fall back to the bottom when the passage exists in both.
  - **Accept:** real Claude Code and Codex sessions at 600 and 1440, scrolled to the top, the middle (anchor on prose, on a card, on a run line) and near the bottom: Work → Conversation → Work keeps the same passage in view each way; `tests/browser/` gains the Work → Conversation cases beside UI-41c's.
  - **Cause and fix (Antigravity with Yash, 14 Nov, PR #485):** in Work mode, top visible elements inside multi-passage tool runs are often ops rows within subsequent folded passages. In Conversation mode, these subsequent passages are hidden (`data-folded="hidden"` with zero height) while only the run's lead passage renders the `.run-line`. UI-41c failed to resolve to the lead's run-line or the passage's prose/turn, and its unmounted target fallback targeted the zero-height placeholder rather than the lead. Additionally, premature re-anchoring in a layout effect overwrote the restored anchor during reflow clamping. Fixed by implementing a complete nearest-element resolution chain across `slot` and `leadSlot` (`ops-row`, `run-line`, `prose`, `turn`), directing unmounted allocations to `lead` if the slot is hidden, persisting the restored anchor to `readingAnchor.current` and memory upon alignment, and eliminating premature re-anchoring.
  - **Verified:** real Claude Code (`rec_dac6d1d1` 3.7 MB, `rec_7baa719e` 390 KB) and Codex (`rec_45333698` 5.4 MB) sessions at 1440 px and 600 px across scroll positions (near top, middle, near bottom) all hold passage alignment both ways (100% matched); `tests/browser/conversation-switch.spec.ts` augmented with top, bottom, and 600 px cases (7/7 passed in 52.7s on Edge).
- [x] **UI-41e · Work → Conversation still jumps to the end of the page** · P0 · `ui` `bug` — Yash, 14 Nov, after UI-41c (#478) and UI-41d (#485) both merged, on a build that includes #485: "the work to conversation still gets to the end of the page."
  - **Reproduction (Fable, current main, the sample workspace `/?demo=`, about 1440x900, `.history` is the scroller):** start in Work at 25% (scrollTop 1592 of 6867, top passage `…toolu_synthetic0001`): Conversation shows scrollTop 829 of 829, the very end, with a different top passage; back in Work it is 829, not 1592. Start at 50% (3442 of 6703, `…toolu_synthetic0009`): Conversation 829 of 829 again; back in Work 829, not 3442. Work → Conversation lands at the bottom; Conversation → Work then keeps the raw pixel offset, not the passage. The demo's conversation is short, so check real sessions too.
  - **Why the specs stay green:** `tests/browser/conversation-switch.spec.ts` (UI-41c and UI-41d) passes on main, so it does not exercise the path a person takes (the real Work and Conversation buttons at a real scroll position). Find out why first: clicks against store calls, which timing, whether `.history` is the measured element, and whether later merges changed the path (UI-48 #492 turned unloaded passages into `HistoryWaitCard`, LIVE-7/7b changed how the live projection reloads, UI-47 added wait notices).
  - **Where:** `apps/ui/src/components/History.tsx`: `switching`, `modeAnchor`, the subscribe layout effect that captures the anchor before the re-render, and the restore after it. Keep the change to the mode-switch anchor (PERF-4 works on the quiet swap and scroll anchoring in the same file).
  - **Do:** (1) capture the passage at the top of the view plus its offset before the mode changes; (2) restore that passage, or the nearest one that view renders, to the same offset in both directions; (3) fall back to the bottom only when the reader was following the latest; (4) a spec that clicks the real Work and Conversation buttons at real scroll positions on a session long enough that Conversation is longer than the window, red on main and green after.
  - **Accept:** real Claude Code and Codex sessions (`pnpm check:real` in a scratch home on its own port, never 7878) at 600 and 1440, starting near the top, in the middle and near the bottom: Work → Conversation → Work keeps the same passage at the top each way; the sample workspace's round trip above holds its place; the spec covers it; the follow-latest case still goes to the bottom.
  - **Cause and fix (Claude Sonnet 5.5, 14 Nov):** Fable's exact numbers did not reproduce on a fresh sample workspace at nine window sizes; what did reproduce on current main is below, all in `History.tsx`'s mode-switch anchor. (1) When the passage the reader was at is not mounted yet, the fallback placed it at the Work-mode offset: a view 500 to 4,900 px inside a tall run's cards becomes "that many pixels past the one line that stands for the run" in Conversation, which lands on a later turn or at the end of the page (seen on a real 8.6 MB Claude session). It now uses the same clamp as the mounted case and remembers the place as that passage and offset, so the pass that mounts it does not align the other mode's anchor. (2) Switching back approximated from the approximation: a tall run's cards are one line in Conversation, and a place in the last screen of a short page cannot sit at the top of it, so Work → Conversation → Work drifted (hundreds of pixels, or a turn). The place the reader left is now kept until they scroll, press a navigation key, drag the scrollbar or open a run, and a switch back with none of those returns to it exactly. (3) At 600 px in a long session the anchor correction could undo itself on every pass (estimated heights give way to measured ones as the window slides), and React ended it with "maximum update depth", a blank "This view could not be displayed" (this is also why `conversation-switch.spec.ts:75` read a null `.history` on main after UI-48 (#492): the view had crashed, not met a wait card). More than 12 corrections in one chain of commits are now refused; the view settles within one correction of the right place.
  - **Verified:** `conversation-switch.spec.ts` gains six cases (25, 50 and 80% of a long-run session at 1440 and 600 px: real wheel, then a scroll deep inside a tall run's cards, then the real Work and Conversation buttons, two round trips, same turn each way and the exact pixel offset back in Work; at the very end of the short Conversation page the turn must still be in view). On main: all six red (offset drift of 484 to 1,905 px, and the blank view at 600 px); after: 18 of 18 across three repeats, the file's 13 cases green twice, `conversation-view`, `conversation-scroll`, `b4-history`, `ship-2f-history`, `passage-layout` green. Real sessions in a scratch home, Follow off through its real switch, 1440 and 600 px, five places from near the top to near the bottom: Claude Code `rec_042c67ca` (16.9 MB) and `rec_22688921` (16.3 MB), Codex `rec_7f1936ab` (7.7 MB) and `rec_45333698` (5.4 MB): 40 of 40 round trips on the same turn each way, exact in Work to within 3 px (except where Conversation's own end of page clamps, where the turn stays in view). The sample workspace's round trip now returns to the exact scroll position both ways; the follow-latest case still goes to the bottom (existing case). Not run: a live, growing session during the switch.

- [x] **UI-47 · Loading messages that match the wait** · P0 · `ui` `perf` — Yash, 14 Nov: "for somethings like git graph, it takes a lot of time to load the first time, so ask the user to wait and not close the window, similarly other components give proper messages according to the state and how much time they take, if less time than they will wait, but if more than proper message."

    - Set the rule from those numbers. Under about 400 ms, show nothing. Up to about 2 s, show the surface's wait card (UI-48). Beyond that, add one plain line saying what is happening and, where it is true, that it is a one-time cost (for example: "Reading this repository's history for the first time. This can take a minute; you can keep working, but leave the window open."). Show real progress only when it is measured, never a fake bar.
    - A failure says what failed and offers a retry.

    - Every surface in the list follows the rule, checked on real sessions at 600 and 1440.

  - **Done (14 Nov, Antigravity):** measured cold and repeat load times across all 10 surfaces (Git graph 3,231 ms cold / 875 ms repeat; Changes 922 ms; Compare 389 ms; Files 313 ms cold / 6 ms repeat; peek 18 ms; Workspace 17 ms; search 4–8 ms; large session 15 ms; Home 252 ms; Usage 5 ms). Implemented shared hook `useWaitNotice` and token-compliant component `WaitNotice` (<400ms silent, 400ms–2s quiet placeholder, >2s plain explanation noting one-time cost for cold loads, failure state with interactive retry button). Wired across Git graph, Changes, Compare, Files explorer, Peek, Workspace, Search, Large session viewer, Home session overview, and Usage. Playwright browser specs `tests/browser/loading-messages.spec.ts` passed (4 passed at 1440 and 600 px on Edge); Vitest unit tests `apps/ui/test/wait-notice.test.ts` (4 passed) and `apps/ui/test/palette-paint-policy.test.ts` (2 passed). Visual frames captured at 1440 and 600 px in `docs/review/ui-47-loading-messages.md` and artifact gallery. Checked on real sessions: Codex `rec_fa9e37daee9f58c4` (1735 obs), Claude Code `rec_5ee6b391f452bd9c` (2400 obs), plus `check:real` cases (`local-branches`, `workspace-files`, `usage-context` across 258 real sessions).
